What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, and I just don’t know what to do first… then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to concentrate or make decisions than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like anything around me can pull my mind away. If the television is on in the next room, or if two people start talking near me at the senior center, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place in what I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with concentration and organization?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is a good word for it. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click my pen or keep shifting in my chair, and then I notice people looking over at me. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt guilty about before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. first until the whole day is gone.\n- key: S006\n  name: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\n  description: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n  manifestation: When there’s a form to fill out, insurance papers to read, or a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off because my head feels worn out before I even start.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m forever hunting for things I just had in my hand, like the car keys, my reading glasses, appointment cards, recipes for the church supper, or the folder I need to take to the doctor’s office.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If the television is on in the next room or two people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just follows that instead, and then I lose my place in what I was writing or planning.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve missed returning calls, left bills sitting under a stack of mail, forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to sit politely in a meeting, I catch myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around so much that people look over at me.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: During volunteer meetings or even at supper, I keep getting up to check the calendar, look for a paper, refill water, or just move around, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated and listen.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a little girl, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, forgot books and homework, and needed reminders to finish seatwork before I could go outside.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, and I just don’t know what to do first… then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to concentrate or make decisions than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like anything around me can pull my mind away. If the television is on in the next room, or if two people start talking near me at the senior center, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place in what I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with concentration and organization?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is a good word for it. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I find little reasons to do something else because my head feels tired before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. I usually hesitate more than jump into things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks that you used to handle with ease?\n\nPatient: Yes. But lately it’s gotten bad enough that I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, G31.84\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, and I just don’t know what to do first… then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to concentrate or make decisions than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like anything around me can pull my mind away. If the television is on in the next room, or if two people start talking near me at the senior center, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place in what I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with concentration and organization?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is a good word for it. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click my pen or keep shifting in my chair, and then I notice people looking over at me. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt guilty about before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming or difficult to manage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I find little reasons to do something else because my head feels tired before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. I usually hesitate more than jump into things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks that you used to handle with ease?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to be able to manage things like bills, appointments, and organizing activities without much trouble. But lately it’s gotten bad enough that I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center. My daughter has started double-checking my bills and appointments now because she doesn’t really trust that I can keep it all straight by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with finding the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might forget a name here and there like anyone does, but I haven’t noticed trouble finding words when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your ability to participate in or enjoy activities you used to find pleasurable or fulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that. I still enjoy the things I like when I’m actually doing them. I guess I haven’t noticed a big change in that, not like losing interest altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily routines and tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really why I came in. I can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. My kitchen table is just covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I’ll sit there trying to figure out what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone and I haven’t really done any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming or difficult to manage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I find little reasons to do something else because my head feels tired before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. I usually hesitate more than jump into things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks that you used to handle with ease?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to be able to manage things like bills, appointments, and organizing activities without much trouble. But lately it’s gotten bad enough that I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center. My daughter has started double-checking my bills and appointments now because she doesn’t really trust that I can keep it all straight by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with finding the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might forget a name here and there like anyone does, but I haven’t noticed trouble finding words when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your ability to participate in or enjoy activities you used to find pleasurable or fulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that. I still enjoy the things I like when I’m actually doing them. I guess I haven’t noticed a big change in that, not like losing interest altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily routines and tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really why I came in. I can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. My kitchen table is just covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I’ll sit there trying to figure out what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone and I haven’t really done any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been withdrawing from social activities or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I still see my family and talk to people when things come up. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click my pen or keep shifting in my chair, and then I notice people looking over at me. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt guilty about before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming or difficult to manage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I find little reasons to do something else because my head feels tired before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. I usually hesitate more than jump into things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks that you used to handle with ease?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to be able to manage things like bills, appointments, and organizing activities without much trouble. But lately it’s gotten bad enough that I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center. My daughter has started double-checking my bills and appointments now because she doesn’t really trust that I can keep it all straight by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with finding the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might forget a name here and there like anyone does, but I haven’t noticed trouble finding words when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your ability to participate in or enjoy activities you used to find pleasurable or fulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that. I still enjoy the things I like when I’m actually doing them. I guess I haven’t noticed a big change in that, not like losing interest altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily routines and tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. That’s really why I came in. I can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. My kitchen table is just covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I’ll sit there trying to figure out what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone and I haven’t really done any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been withdrawing from social activities or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I still see my family and talk to people when things come up. I may be a little less active socially than I used to be, but I haven’t really noticed myself pulling away on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed it in day-to-day things. I miss returning calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail that I meant to take care of. I’ve forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to manage better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine presence of significant cognitive decline", "question": "Have you noticed any problems with understanding or following instructions?"}

I’ll sit there trying to figure out what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone and I haven’t really done any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been withdrawing from social activities or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I still see my family and talk to people when things come up. I may be a little less active socially than I used to be, but I haven’t really noticed myself pulling away on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed it in day-to-day things. I miss returning calls, or I’ll find a bill sitting under a stack of mail that I meant to take care of. I’ve forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once I realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to manage better.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with understanding or following instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say understanding them is the problem. If someone tells me something clearly, I usually know what they mean. Following through on the steps can be harder, especially if there are a lot of details, but I haven’t really thought of it as not understanding instructions.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It feels like anything around me can pull my mind away. If the television is on in the next room, or if two people start talking near me at the senior center, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place in what I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with concentration and organization?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is a good word for it. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click my pen or keep shifting in my chair, and then I notice people looking over at me. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt guilty about before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming or difficult to manage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. My kitchen table is covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I sit down to sort it out, and I just don’t know what to do first… then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to concentrate or make decisions than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It feels like anything around me can pull my mind away. If the television is on in the next room, or if two people start talking near me at the senior center, I start listening without meaning to. Then I look back down and I’ve lost my place in what I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with concentration and organization?\n\nPatient: Yes… overwhelmed is a good word for it. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all those papers spread out—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior-center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish—and I just freeze up. I can’t figure out what to handle first, and then hours pass and I haven’t really gotten anything done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say sad, exactly. Mostly I feel frustrated and worn out by all of it. I haven’t really noticed feeling down in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that you would normally be able to recall?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s everyday things, mostly. I’ll forget to return phone calls, or I’ll find a bill still sitting under a stack of mail when I thought I’d taken care of it. I’ve also forgotten errands I told my daughter I would do. And more than once, I’ve realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments. It’s upsetting because these are things I used to keep track of better.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click my pen or keep shifting in my chair, and then I notice people looking over at me. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you wouldn't have felt guilty about before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I feel bad when I forget something or let something slip, but I wouldn’t say I’m feeling guilty about things I normally wouldn’t. It’s more frustration than guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a big change in learning new things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always try to take on much new stuff these days, so it’s hard for me to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I do that a lot, but I may not always notice it. If I’m excited or trying to remember what I want to say, I might jump in, but I can’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed happening often.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because they feel too overwhelming or difficult to manage?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. If it’s something like filling out a form, reading through insurance papers, or trying to put a program schedule together, I’ll keep putting it off. I find little reasons to do something else because my head feels tired before I even start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially impulsive, at least not in a way I’ve noticed. I usually hesitate more than jump into things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to perform daily tasks that you used to handle with ease?\n\nPatient: Yes. I used to be able to manage things like bills, appointments, and organizing activities without much trouble. But lately it’s gotten bad enough that I had to give up leading a big event at the senior center. My daughter has started double-checking my bills and appointments now because she doesn’t really trust that I can keep it all straight by myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with finding the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might forget a name here and there like anyone does, but I haven’t noticed trouble finding words when I’m talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant change in your ability to participate in or enjoy activities you used to find pleasurable or fulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say that. I still enjoy the things I like when I’m actually doing them. My kitchen table is just covered with my husband’s appointment papers, church flyers, notes from the senior center, and half-finished lists. I’ll sit there trying to figure out what to do first, and then somehow the whole day is gone and I haven’t really done any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been withdrawing from social activities or spending less time with friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way.
